---------- turn 17 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I keep getting these moments where I’m right back there, seeing my cousin on the pavement. It just comes out of nowhere. I’ll be in the church office, trying to answer emails or talk to someone like everything’s normal, and suddenly I can hear the shouting again, see the lights flashing like it’s happening right in front of me.\n\nSorry, I know that’s probably not a very organized answer. I just haven’t been able to shake it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these moments of seeing your cousin on the pavement happen to you?\n\nPatient: It happens a lot… I mean, I’m not sure I could give you a clean number. It’s like I’ll just be standing in the church office, trying to act normal, answering emails or talking to someone, and then out of nowhere I’m seeing him on the pavement again. Hearing the shouting, seeing the lights, like I’m back in that moment instead of where I actually am.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these moments of seeing your cousin on the pavement?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since my cousin was killed. That was, um, two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still count it that way—like people count a baby’s age in months or something, which sounds strange, I know. But yeah… it’s been going on that whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities because they remind you of your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I tell myself it’s just traffic or I need the walk, but I’ll take the long way around town so I don’t have to pass the community center or that court. Even if it adds time.\n\nAnd if somebody starts talking about that night, I just… I can’t stay in it. I’ll shut it down, change the subject, or suddenly remember I’ve got something to do somewhere else. Which, as a clergy person, you’d think I’d be better at sitting with hard things, you know? People come to me with all kinds of pain. But with that… I just leave.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping or find yourself easily startled?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… sleep is kind of hard to talk about because my schedule’s weird anyway, with church stuff and late calls sometimes. Like last week someone needed me to come by after a family argument, and then I got home and just sat there thinking about whether I’d said the right thing, so it’s hard to separate what’s normal from what’s… not normal.\n\nAs far as being startled, I mean, people joke that I’m jumpy, but I don’t really know if it’s a “thing” or just me being tired. I guess I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about your cousin's death or the memories associated with it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I’m avoiding thinking about it,” exactly. It’s more like I’ll suddenly realize I’ve taken the longer route through town just so I don’t have to go past the community center or that court.\n\nAnd if somebody brings up that night, I kind of… I just shut it down. Change the subject, or I remember I need to make a call, or I’ve got to check on something at the church. Which is funny, not funny, but—people expect me to be able to sit with grief and hard conversations. I can do it for other people. But when it’s about him, I find a reason to leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I used to love preaching, like really love it—not just the Sunday part, but the whole sitting with a passage and finding the thread in it. And playing keys, too. I could sit there for hours, just messing around with chords.\n\nI used to take pictures all the time, little things around town, light coming through a window or kids playing after service… and I used to run with the guys on the court. Not even serious games, just being out there.\n\nNow it’s like… I still do some of it because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to. But it feels like I’m going through the motions. And some of it I just avoid altogether. It doesn’t feel alive in me the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or blame yourself in any way for what happened to your cousin?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know people say you can’t think like that, and I’ve said that to other people a hundred times, you know, standing in hospital rooms or after funerals, trying to help them not carry what isn’t theirs.\n\nBut with him, I keep going back over it. Like if I had ended the event earlier… if I’d just said, “Alright, everybody go home,” or if I had walked him out myself instead of getting caught up talking to someone. Or if I’d been paying closer attention to those boys, keeping better watch, maybe… maybe he’d still be here.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like I’m trying to make myself more important than I was or something, but it just sits in my head. I can’t stop seeing all the little moments where I could’ve done one thing different.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m doing something completely ordinary, like setting up chairs or looking over notes, and then I’m right back there thinking, if I had just ended the event earlier… if I’d said, “Everybody go home now,” maybe none of it would’ve happened.\n\nOr I think I should’ve walked him out myself. I keep getting stuck on that. Like I was supposed to be watching over people, you know? That’s kind of what folks expect from me anyway, at church, in the community… and I keep thinking if I had kept a better eye on those boys, if I’d noticed something sooner, my cousin might still be here.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s not every once in a while. It’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt over your cousin's death is overwhelming at times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelming is probably the right word.\n\nIt’s not like it just comes in one clean thought, like “I feel guilty,” and then I can pray it off or talk myself down. Most days it’s like this whole mix sitting in my chest from the time I wake up—fear, anger, guilt… all tangled together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be doing something stupid, like trying to find my keys or checking if the church lights got turned off—because people always leave that back hallway light on, I don’t know why—and suddenly it’s right there again. Like I failed God. Failed my family. Failed those kids who trusted me to keep things steady.\n\nSo yeah… sometimes it feels bigger than I can carry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not naming it as guilt, you know? Like if somebody at church is asking me about the bulletin or the sound system acts up—because it always does at the worst time, I swear—but it’s still there underneath.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s already sitting in my chest, that mix of fear and anger and guilt. Like I failed God, failed my family… failed those kids who trusted me to keep things safe. So I don’t know if I get whole days without it. Maybe a few minutes here and there, but not days.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you have completely stopped doing since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are a few.\n\nI used to love preaching. Like, not just doing it because it was my role, but really feeling it, you know? Sitting with the scripture, getting excited about how to say something in a way people could actually carry with them. Now I still do it if I have to, but it feels like I’m just… pushing words out.\n\nAnd playing keys too. I used to get lost in that. Sometimes after service I’d stay behind and just play, even if nobody was there. The old upright at the church has this one sticky key—middle C, I think—and I used to joke that it had more attitude than half the choir. But now I barely touch it unless I’m needed.\n\nTaking pictures, too. I used to always have my camera out at events, catching little moments. And basketball with the guys… running games, talking trash, laughing. I don’t really go anymore.\n\nIt’s like those things are still there, but none of it feels alive in me the way it used to. So I either avoid it, or I go through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have not completely stopped doing, but now find you are doing less often since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably more accurate for some of it. Like I haven’t totally stopped preaching because, I mean, people still expect me to show up on Sunday. The bulletin doesn’t print itself, and Miss Carla will definitely let you know if the announcements are in the wrong order—she’s got eyes like a hawk.\n\nBut I do it less in the way I used to. I don’t sit with it the same. I don’t feel that… spark, I guess. Same with playing keys. I’ll play if somebody needs me to, but I’m not staying after service just to mess around anymore.\n\nTaking pictures too—I still might take one if someone asks, but I’m not walking around looking for moments like I used to. And basketball with the guys… I haven’t fully cut them off, but I barely go run with them now. It just all feels flat. Like I’m there, but not really in it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have not completely stopped doing but now find you are doing less often since your cousin's death because they no longer give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s the part of it. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or whatever. I used to love preaching, like really love it, and playing keys after service, taking pictures, running with the guys on the court… all of that used to give me something.\n\nNow I still do some of it sometimes, but it feels like I’m just moving my hands or saying the words. Like when you know the song but you’re not really singing it, you’re just… making the sounds. And basketball, I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m gonna go, then I end up not going because I already know I won’t feel it.\n\nSo yeah, I do them less because they don’t feel alive in me anymore. I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable but now find you have lost interest in altogether since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, altogether is a strong word, but honestly, it feels like that with a lot of things.\n\nPreaching used to be something I loved. Same with playing keys—sometimes I’d just sit there after everybody left and play whatever came to me. Taking pictures, too. I used to notice little things, like the way light hit the fellowship hall windows or somebody laughing when they thought nobody was looking. And basketball with the guys… that used to be easy for me to show up to.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are there, but I don’t reach for them. If I do them, I’m mostly just going through the motions. None of it feels alive in me anymore, so I either avoid it or I do the bare minimum and get out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, almost all the stuff that used to feel like me, you know?\n\nPreaching, playing keys, taking pictures, running with the guys on the court… I used to actually look forward to that stuff. Like with the keys, I could lose a whole hour after church just messing around, and somebody would be turning lights off around me like, “You planning to go home?” But now… I don’t know. I might still do it if I have to, or if somebody asks, but it’s like my body is there and the rest of me didn’t show up.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say I’ve lost interest in most of it. I mostly just go through the motions, or I don’t do it at all, because none of it feels alive in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Since my cousin was killed… so, that’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nIt’s weird because time does that thing where it feels like yesterday and also like it’s been forever. I remember what I was wearing that week, and then I can’t remember whole stretches after that, like church events people say I was at and I’m like, was I? But yeah… the losing interest part, that started after he was killed and it’s just been going on since then.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Most days, honestly. It’s not like it comes and goes clean. I wake up and it’s already sitting in my chest—this mix of guilt, and fear, and anger.

I guess I haven’t really kept track of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about your cousin's death or the memories associated with it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t sit there and think, “I’m avoiding thinking about it,” exactly. Change the subject, or I remember I need to make a call, or I’ve got to check on something at the church. I used to love preaching, like really love it—not just the Sunday part, but the whole sitting with a passage and finding the thread in it. And playing keys, too. I could sit there for hours, just messing around with chords.\n\nI used to take pictures all the time, little things around town, light coming through a window or kids playing after service… and I used to run with the guys on the court. Not even serious games, just being out there.\n\nNow it’s like… I still do some of it because I’m supposed to, or because people expect me to. But it feels like I’m going through the motions. And some of it I just avoid altogether. It doesn’t feel alive in me the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or blame yourself in any way for what happened to your cousin?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know people say you can’t think like that, and I’ve said that to other people a hundred times, you know, standing in hospital rooms or after funerals, trying to help them not carry what isn’t theirs.\n\nBut with him, I keep going back over it. Like if I had ended the event earlier… if I’d just said, “Alright, everybody go home,” or if I had walked him out myself instead of getting caught up talking to someone. Or if I’d been paying closer attention to those boys, keeping better watch, maybe… maybe he’d still be here.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like I’m trying to make myself more important than I was or something, but it just sits in my head. I can’t stop seeing all the little moments where I could’ve done one thing different.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: A lot. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’ll come up when I’m doing something completely ordinary, like setting up chairs or looking over notes, and then I’m right back there thinking, if I had just ended the event earlier… if I’d said, “Everybody go home now,” maybe none of it would’ve happened.\n\nOr I think I should’ve walked him out myself. I keep getting stuck on that. Like I was supposed to be watching over people, you know? That’s kind of what folks expect from me anyway, at church, in the community… and I keep thinking if I had kept a better eye on those boys, if I’d noticed something sooner, my cousin might still be here.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s not every once in a while. It’s all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your guilt over your cousin's death is overwhelming at times?\n\nPatient: Yeah… overwhelming is probably the right word.\n\nIt’s not like it just comes in one clean thought, like “I feel guilty,” and then I can pray it off or talk myself down. Most days it’s like this whole mix sitting in my chest from the time I wake up—fear, anger, guilt… all tangled together.\n\nAnd then I’ll be doing something stupid, like trying to find my keys or checking if the church lights got turned off—because people always leave that back hallway light on, I don’t know why—and suddenly it’s right there again. Like I failed God. Failed my family. Failed those kids who trusted me to keep things steady.\n\nSo yeah… sometimes it feels bigger than I can carry.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't feel guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe there are moments where I’m distracted enough that I’m not naming it as guilt, you know? Like if somebody at church is asking me about the bulletin or the sound system acts up—because it always does at the worst time, I swear—but it’s still there underneath.\n\nMost days I wake up and it’s already sitting in my chest, that mix of fear and anger and guilt. Like I failed God, failed my family… failed those kids who trusted me to keep things safe. So I don’t know if I get whole days without it. Maybe a few minutes here and there, but not days.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you have completely stopped doing since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there are a few.\n\nI used to love preaching. Like, not just doing it because it was my role, but really feeling it, you know? Sitting with the scripture, getting excited about how to say something in a way people could actually carry with them. Now I still do it if I have to, but it feels like I’m just… pushing words out.\n\nAnd playing keys too. I used to get lost in that. Sometimes after service I’d stay behind and just play, even if nobody was there. So I either avoid it, or I go through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have not completely stopped doing, but now find you are doing less often since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably more accurate for some of it. Like I haven’t totally stopped preaching because, I mean, people still expect me to show up on Sunday. The bulletin doesn’t print itself, and Miss Carla will definitely let you know if the announcements are in the wrong order—she’s got eyes like a hawk.\n\nBut I do it less in the way I used to. I don’t sit with it the same. I don’t feel that… spark, I guess. Same with playing keys. I’ll play if somebody needs me to, but I’m not staying after service just to mess around anymore.\n\nTaking pictures too—I still might take one if someone asks, but I’m not walking around looking for moments like I used to. And basketball with the guys… I haven’t fully cut them off, but I barely go run with them now. It just all feels flat. Like I’m there, but not really in it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you have not completely stopped doing but now find you are doing less often since your cousin's death because they no longer give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s the part of it. It’s not just that I’m busy or tired or whatever. I used to love preaching, like really love it, and playing keys after service, taking pictures, running with the guys on the court… all of that used to give me something.\n\nNow I still do some of it sometimes, but it feels like I’m just moving my hands or saying the words. Like when you know the song but you’re not really singing it, you’re just… making the sounds. And basketball, I mean, I’ll tell myself I’m gonna go, then I end up not going because I already know I won’t feel it.\n\nSo yeah, I do them less because they don’t feel alive in me anymore. I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them altogether.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to find pleasurable but now find you have lost interest in altogether since your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, altogether is a strong word, but honestly, it feels like that with a lot of things.\n\nPreaching used to be something I loved. Same with playing keys—sometimes I’d just sit there after everybody left and play whatever came to me. Taking pictures, too. I used to notice little things, like the way light hit the fellowship hall windows or somebody laughing when they thought nobody was looking. And basketball with the guys… that used to be easy for me to show up to.\n\nNow it’s like… I know those things are there, but I don’t reach for them. If I do them, I’m mostly just going through the motions. None of it feels alive in me anymore, so I either avoid it or I do the bare minimum and get out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, almost all the stuff that used to feel like me, you know?\n\nPreaching, playing keys, taking pictures, running with the guys on the court… I used to actually look forward to that stuff. Like with the keys, I could lose a whole hour after church just messing around, and somebody would be turning lights off around me like, “You planning to go home?” But now… I don’t know. I might still do it if I have to, or if somebody asks, but it’s like my body is there and the rest of me didn’t show up.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say I’ve lost interest in most of it. I mostly just go through the motions, or I don’t do it at all, because none of it feels alive in me anymore.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you felt like you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Since my cousin was killed… so, that’s been about two years and three months now.\n\nIt’s weird because time does that thing where it feels like yesterday and also like it’s been forever. I remember what I was wearing that week, and then I can’t remember whole stretches after that, like church events people say I was at and I’m like, was I? But yeah… the losing interest part, that started after he was killed and it’s just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling guilty about your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Most days, honestly. It’s not like it comes and goes clean. And then I’ll be trying to do something simple, like make coffee or get ready for church, and my mind goes off thinking about who I should’ve been, what I should’ve done, and—sorry, I know you asked how long. It’s been like that since he died, but it feels especially like… most days now, from the time I wake up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
